10-14                                                            DERIVATIVE SECURITIES




Banks as         In the example, we said that LDM and XYZ arranged the swap through
intermediaries   an intermediary. Most companies lack the resources and expertise to
                 find suitable counterparties and evaluate their credit-worthiness. A
                 large, money-center bank will act as an intermediary in most swap
                 transactions. The bank serves as a clearinghouse for the interest
                 payments and guarantees performance by each counterparty.

                 From the company's perspective, the swap is essentially made with
                 the bank — it may not know or even care who the counterparty is.
                 Of course, the bank charges a fee for providing these services,
                 usually in the form of a few basis points added to the overall rate that
                 is paid in the swap. The bank usually receives a fee from both
                 counterparties, so the all-in-cost of the interest payments for each
                 company will include the fees paid to the bank.


                 Currency Swaps

Exchange of      A currency swap is an exchange of cash flows denominated in one
cash flows in    currency for the cash flows in a different currency. The driving force
different        behind the swap might be that a company may be able to borrow in one
currencies
                 currency at a lower rate than in another currency. Let's look at
                 an illustration that shows how a currency swap might work.

Example          Consider a German company and a U.S. corporation that are trying to
                 raise financing for their respective businesses in their home
                 currencies. The German company has borrowed in the Deutsche mark
                 (DM) bond market extensively in the past few months. This means that
                 there could be a relative overabundance of DM-denominated debt
                 issued by German companies and investors' demand is low. However,
                 there is relatively little DM-denominated debt issued by U.S.
                 corporations in that market. This situation may mean that a U.S.
                 company could issue bonds at a lower rate than the German firm in the
                 DM market.




                                        v-1.1                                 v.05/13/94
                                                                              p.01/14/00
DERIVATIVE SECURITIES                                                                    10-15




                        In the US$ market, investors perceive that the German company is a
                        better credit risk than the U.S. corporation, and the German
                        company could issue bonds at a lower rate in the US$ market. This is
                        an opportunity for a swap to occur between the two companies. The
                        German company could issue in the US$ market and the U.S.
                        corporation could issue in the DM market. The two companies
                        would exchange initial receipts from the bond issues (they would
                        most likely be equal) and the U.S. company would then pay the
                        interest and repay the principal of the US$-denominated bonds
                        issued by the German company. The German company would do
                        likewise on the DM-denominated bonds.

                        Remember that the swap only makes sense if both companies can
                        lower their all-in-cost of borrowing funds. Also remember that a
                        bank or some other intermediary will usually be part of the
                        arrangement and will charge fees for its service.



UNIT SUMMARY

                        An option is a contract between a buyer and a seller. A call option
                        gives the buyer the right to buy an asset. A put option gives the buyer
                        the right to sell an asset. The underlying asset is the specific asset
                        named in the option contract. Exercising the option is either the
                        buying or the selling of the underlying asset.

                        An option is in the money when it produces a profit at the time of
                        exercise. An option is out of the money when it does not produce a
                        profit at the time of exercise.

                        An American option gives the right to exercise the option at any
                        time up to the expiration date. European option gives the right to
                        exercise the option only on the expiration date.

                        The Option Clearing Corporation (OCC) is the clearinghouse for
                        options trading.




v.05/13/94                                 v-1.1
p.01/14/00
10-16                                                                 DERIVATIVE SECURITIES




                     Call holders and put writers post margin money with brokers to
                     assure performance of investors' obligations. A margin call requires
                     the deposit of additional funds into the margin account.

                     A swap is an exchange of cash flows between two counterparties.

                     An interest rate swap is used by a company to meet the need for
                     fixed or floating interest rates at lower rates than may be available in
                     the market.

                     A foreign currency swap is a method for companies to borrow at a
                     lower rate in one currency than in another currency.

                     Swaps are arranged through an intermediary and are intended to
                     reduce the borrowing costs for both counterparties.

Congratulations! You have completed the Basics of Corporate Finance course. Please
complete the final Progress Check which follows to check your understanding of
securities.

You have studied the basic concepts and calculations that are the foundation for future
courses related to corporate finance and the capital and derivative markets. You should
now have a strong working vocabulary for the financial world and an understanding of the
basic types of analyses used in the corporate finance field. The index and glossary are
provided to help you use this workbook as an easy reference in the future. Use it as you
pursue more advanced courses in the corporate finance series.

4. Select a condition that may allow an interest rate swap between two companies to be
   profitable.

       _____ a) Floating rates are expected to rise because of inflation.
       _____ b) The credit rate differential spread between fixed and floating rates for
                the two companies is significant.
       _____ c) There is a significant difference between U.S. Treasury bill and U.S.
                Treasury bond rates.

3. The only loss sustained by the holder of a call that expires "out of the money" is the:

              c) premium paid for the call option.

              The holder of an option is only liable for the price paid for the option. If the option
              expires "out of the money," he is not required to exercise the option and incur
              additional losses.




4. Select a condition that may allow an interest rate swap between two companies to be
   profitable:

              b) The credit rate differential spread between fixed and floating rates
                 for the two companies is significant.

              This condition may allow both companies to lower their cost of debt, provided
              that one needs floating-rate and the other needs fixed-rate. Also the
              calculations must include the intermediary and its fees.

              pay Mega fixed rate payments of 9%; Mega agrees to pay Super floating rate
              payments of LIBOR + 0.75%. How much would each company save on the

6. Consider the rates available in the market to Mega Corporation and Super, Inc.

                                     Fixed Rate      Floating Rate
              Mega Corporation          9.00%        LIBOR + 1.5%
              Super, Inc.             11.25%         LIBOR + 2.25%


              Currently, Mega Corporation is paying 9.00% to bondholders, but it wants
              to replace this fixed rate financing with floating rate financing. Super, Inc.
              pays LIBOR + 2.25% to investors in need of fixed rate financing. Super
              agrees to pay Mega fixed rate payments of 9%; Mega agrees to pay Super
              floating rate payments of LIBOR + 0.75%. How much would each company
              save on the financing it needs by entering into a fixed / floating rate swap
              transaction?

              Mega        0.75%      a
              Super       0.75%      a
              Cash Flow                     Mega Corporation            Super, Inc.
              Payment to investors                   - 9.00%          - LIBOR + 2.25%
              Pays in swap                   - LIBOR + 0.75%                    - 9.00%
              Receives in swap                       +9.00%           +LIBOR + 0.75%
              Net payment                    - LIBOR + 0.75%                   - 10.50%


              Rate available in market        LIBOR + 1.50% Floating          11.25% Fixed
              Net payment in swap             LIBOR + 0.75%           a       10.50%         a
              Savings                                  0.75% Floating          0.75% Fixed

              Both companies save 0.75% by entering into an interest rate swap to get the
              type of funding they need.

GLOSSARY


  Accrued Interest    The amount of interest accumulated on a debt security between
                      interest paying dates

  Acid-test Ratio     See quick ratio

  Annuity             A series of payments or deposits of equal size spaced evenly over
                      a specified period of time

  Annuity Due         Annuity where the payments are to be made at the beginning of
                      each period

  Asset-specific      The amount of total risk that can be eliminated by diversification by
  Risk                creating a portfolio. Also known as company-specific risk or
                      unsystematic risk.

  Average             Average number of days necessary to receive cash for the sale of
  Collection Period   a company's products. It is calculated by dividing the value of the
                      accounts receivable by the average daily sales for the period.

  Basic Earnings      Percentage of earnings relative to total assets; indication of how
  Power Ratio         effectively assets are used to generate earnings. It is calculated by
                      dividing earnings before interest and taxes by the book value of all
                      assets.

  Basis Point         One one-hundredth of one percent

  Benchmark           A standard by which something may be compared and measured

  Beta                A measure of the riskiness of a specific security compared to the
                      riskiness of the market as a whole; measure of the systematic risk
                      of a security or a portfolio of securities




v.05/13/94                          v-1.1
p.01/14/00
G-2                                                                             GLOSSARY




  Bond                A long-term debt instrument in which the issuer (borrower) is
                      obligated to pay the investor (lender) a specified amount of
                      money, usually at specific intervals, and to repay the principal
                      amount of the loan at maturity. The periodic payments are based
                      on the rate of interest agreed upon at the time the instrument is
                      sold.

  Bond Equivalent     Bond yield calculated on an annual percentage rate method
  Yield

  Book Value          The value of an asset as carried on the balance sheet of a
                      company. In reference to the value of a company, it is the net worth
                      (equity) of the company.

  Book Value          The book value of a company divided by the number of shares
  per Share           outstanding

  Call Option         A contract that gives the holder the right to buy an asset for a
                      specified price on or before a given expiration (maturity) date

  Capital Asset       A model for estimating equilibrium rates of return and values of
  Pricing Model       assets in financial markets; uses beta as a measure of asset risk
  (CAPM)              relative to market risk

  Capital Budgeting   The process of ranking and selecting investment alternatives and
                      capital expenditures

  Capital Market      A market that specializes in trading long-term, relatively high risk
                      securities

  Capital Structure   The combination of debt, preferred stock, and common stock used
                      by a company to provide capital for the purchase of its fixed
                      assets

  Common Stock        A financial security that represents an ownership claim on the
                      assets and earnings of a company. This claim is valid after the
                      claims of the debt providers and preferred stockholders have been
                      satisfied.

  Company-            See asset-specific risk
  specific Risk


                                    v-1.1                                       v.05/13/94
                                                                                p.01/14/00
GLOSSARY                                                                              G-3




  Compound          Interest paid on principal and on interest earned in previous
  Interest          periods

  Continuous        The process of continuously adding interest to a principal plus
  Compounding       interest amount and calculating the resulting compound amount

  Continuous        The process of calculating the present value of a stream of future
  Discounting       cash flows by discounting over a continuous period of time

  Correlation       A measure of the tendency of two variables to change values
  Coefficient       together

  Cost of Capital   The minimum rate of return a company must earn in order to meet
                    the rate of return required by the investors (providers of capital) of
                    the company

  Cost of           The rate of return required by the investors in the common stock of
  Common Stock      the company. A component of the cost of capital.

  Cost of Debt      The cost of debt (bonds, loans, etc.) that a company is charged for
                    borrowing funds. A component of the cost of capital.

  Cost of Equity    Same as the cost of common stock. Sometimes viewed as the
                    rate of return stockholders require to maintain the market value of
                    the company's common stock.

  Cost of           The rate of return required by the investors in the preferred stock of
  Preferred Stock   a company. A component of the cost of capital.

  Coupon(s)         The periodic interest payment(s) made by the issuer of a bond
                    (debt security). Calculated by multiplying the face value of the
                    security by the coupon rate.

  Coupon Rate       The rate of interest paid on a debt security. Generally stated on an
                    annual basis, even if the payments are made at some other
                    interval.




v.05/13/94                        v-1.1
p.01/14/00
G-4                                                                                GLOSSARY




  Current Ratio     A measure of the ability of a company to use its current assets to
                    pay its current liabilities. It is calculated by dividing the total current
                    assets by the total current liabilities.

  Debt Ratio        The percentage of debt that is used in the total capitalization of a
                    company. It is calculated by dividing the total book value of the
                    debt by the book value of all assets.

  Derivitive        An instrument with a value that is derived from the value of the
                    underlying security

  Discount Rate     The rate of interest used to calculate the present value of a stream
                    of future cash flows

  Discounting       The process of calculating the present value of a stream of future
                    cash flows

  Discrete          The process of adding interest to a principal plus interest amount
  Compounding       and calculating the resulting compound amount at specific
                    intervals, such as monthly or annually

  Diversification   The process of spreading a portfolio over many investments to
                    avoid excessive exposure to any one source of risk

  Duration          The weighted average of the time until maturity of each of the
                    expected cash flows of a debt security

  Earnings per      A measure of the earnings generated by a company on a per
  Share             share basis. It is calculated by dividing income available for
                    distribution to shareholders by the number of common shares
                    outstanding.

  Effective         Annualized rate of return on a security computed using compound
  Annual Yield      interest techniques

  Effective         The rate of interest actually earned on an investment. It is
  Interest Rate     calculated as the ratio of the total amount of interest actually
                    earned for one year divided by the amount of the principal.




                                   v-1.1                                          v.05/13/94
                                                                                  p.01/14/00
GLOSSARY                                                                            G-5




  Eurobond         A debt security issued in a market other than the home market of
                   the company issuing the security

  Expected Value   The value of the possible outcomes of a variable weighted by the
                   probabilities of each outcome

  Face Value       The nominal value of a security. Also called the par value.

  Fixed Assets     A measure of the utilization of a company's fixed assets to
  Turnover         generate sales. It is calculated by dividing the sales for the period
  Ratio            by the book value of the net fixed assets.

  Fixed Charge     A measure of how well a company is able to meet its fixed
  Coverage         charges (interest and lease payments) based on the cash
  Ratio            generated by its operations. It is calculated by dividing the
                   earnings before interest and taxes by the total interest charges
                   and lease payments incurred by the firm.

  Free Cash        The funds available for distribution to the capital providers of the
  Flow             company after investments inside the company have been made

  Future Value     The amount a given payment, or series of payments, will be worth
                   at the end of a specified time period, if invested at a given rate

  Independent      A situation where an increase (or decrease) in the benefits of one
  Projects         project has no effect on the benefits of another project. Also, a
                   situation where the acceptance of one project does not preclude
                   the acceptance of another project.

  Internal Rate    The discount rate that equates the present value of the net cash
  of Return        inflows with the present value of the net cash outflows
  (IRR)            (investments). The IRR measures the profitability (rate of return) of
                   an investment in a project or security.

  Inventory        Provides a measure of how often a company's inventory is sold or
  Turnover         "turned over" during a period. It is calculated by dividing the sales
  Ratio            figure for the period by the book value of the inventory at the end of
                   the period.




v.05/13/94                       v-1.1
p.01/14/00
G-6                                                                         GLOSSARY




  Investment      The commitment of funds (capital) in anticipation of an increased
                  return of funds at some point in the future

  Liquidation     The net proceeds (after taxes and expenses) of selling the assets
  Value           of a company at fair market prices

  Liquidity       The ease with which assets or securities can be sold for cash on
                  short notice at a fair price

  Market Risk     The amount of total risk that cannot be eliminated by portfolio
                  diversification. The risk inherent in the general economy as a
                  whole. Also known as systemic risk.

  Market to       Measure of the book value of a company on a per share basis. It is
  Book Ratio      calculated by dividing the book value of the company by the
                  number of common shares outstanding.

  Maturity        The date or the number of days until a security is due to be paid or
                  a loan is to be repaid

  Money Market    A market that specializes in trading short-term, low-risk, very liquid
                  debt securities

  Net Present     The present value of all future cash inflows minus the present value
  Value (NPV)     of all cash outflows

  Nominal         The rate of interest quoted, or stated, to be paid on a security
  Interest Rate

  Operating       Income available after the payment of taxes, plus the value of the
  Cash Flow       non-cash expenses

  Option          See call option and put option

  Ordinary        An annuity where the payments are made at the end of each
  Annuity         period

  Par Value       Nominal value of a security. Same as face value.




                                v-1.1                                       v.05/13/94
                                                                            p.01/14/00
GLOSSARY                                                                                G-7




  Payback            The number of years necessary for the net cash flows of an
  Period             investment to equal the initial cash outlay

  Perpetuity         A special case of an annuity with no set maturity. Payments are
                     made forever.

  Portfolio          A collection of securities and investments held by an investor

  Portfolio          See diversification
  Diversification

  Portfolio Weight   The percentage of a total portfolio represented by a single specific
                     security. It is calculated by dividing the value of the investment in a
                     specific security by the value of the investment in the total portfolio.

  Preferred Stock    A type of equity security where holders have a claim on the assets
                     and earnings of a company after the debt providers but before the
                     holders of common stock. Preferred stock generally pays a fixed
                     or floating rate dividend each year.

  Present Value      The dollar value at the present time (year zero) of a single cash
  (PV)               flow or a stream of future cash flows. The present value is
                     calculated by discounting the future cash flows.

  Price to           A measure of how much investors are willing to pay for each dollar
  Earnings (P/E)     of a company's reported profits. It is calculated by dividing the
  Ratio              market price per share by the earnings per share.

  Primary Market     Market where debt and equity securities are sold by an issuing
                     company to investors to raise capital for its operations

  Probability        A list of all possible outcomes and the chance of each outcome
  Distribution       occurring

  Profit Margin      A measure of how much profit is earned on each dollar of sales. It
  Ratio              is calculated by dividing the net income available for distribution to
                     shareholders by the total sales generated during the period.




v.05/13/94                         v-1.1
p.01/14/00
G-8                                                                           GLOSSARY




  Profitability    A method for determining the profitability of an investment. It is
  Index            calculated by dividing the present value of the future net cash flows
                   by the initial cash investment.

  Project          An investment opportunity for a company

  Put Option       A contract that gives the holder the right to sell an asset for a
                   specified price on or before a given expiration (maturity) date
  Quick Ratio      A measure of how easily a company can use its most liquid current
                   assets to meet its current liabilities. It is calculated by subtracting
                   the book value of the inventories from the total book value of
                   current assets and dividing the result by the total book value of
                   current liabilities. Also known as acid-test ratio.

  Ratio Analysis   The process of using financial ratios, calculated from key accounts
                   found in a company's financial statements, to make judgements
                   concerning the finances and operations of the firm

  Real Interest    The rate of interest paid on an investment adjusted for inflation
  Rate

  Replacement      The amount necessary to duplicate a company's assets at current
  Value            market prices

  Residual Value   The value attributed to a company to represent all future cash flows
                   after the end of the forecast period

  Return on        A measure of the percentage return earned on the value of the
  Common           common equity invested in the company. It is calculated by
  Equity Ratio     dividing the net income available for distribution to shareholders
                   by the book value of the common equity.

  Return on        A measure of the percentage return earned on the value of the
  Total Assets     assets in the company. It is calculated by dividing the net income
  Ratio            available for distribution to shareholders by the book value of all
                   assets.

  Risk Premium     The additional rate of return required on a risky project
                   (investment) when compared to a risk-free project (investment)




                                 v-1.1                                       v.05/13/94
                                                                             p.01/14/00
GLOSSARY                                                                                 G-9




  Risk-free Rate    The rate of return on an investment with known future benefits; a
                    riskless rate of return, often estimated using the return earned on
                    short-term U.S. Treasury securities

  Secondary         The market where securities are exchanged between investors.
  Market            Secondary market transactions have no effect on the issuing
                    company.

  Security          A graph illustrating the equilibrium relationship between the
  Market Line       expected rate of return on securities and their risk as measured by
                    the beta coefficient

  Simple            Interest paid only on the principal; calculated by multiplying the
  Interest          interest rate by the principal

  Standard          A statistical term that measures the dispersion of a variable
  Deviation         around its expected value. The standard deviation is often used as
                    a measure of risk when applied to a return on an investment.

  Swap              An exchange of cash flows between two counterparties. The
                    counterparties may exchange flows in different currencies
                    (currency swap) or exchange floating interest rate payments for
                    fixed rate payments (interest rate swap).

  Systematic Risk   The amount of total risk that cannot be eliminated by portfolio
                    diversification. The risk inherent in the general economy as a
                    whole. Also known as market risk.

  Times Interest    A measure of how well a company is able to meet its interest
  Earned Ratio      payments based on the cash generated by its operations. It is
                    calculated by dividing the earnings before interest and taxes by the
                    total interest charges incurred by the firm.

  Total Asset       A measure of the utilization of all of a company's assets to
  Turnover          generate sales. It is calculated by dividing the sales figure for the
  Ratio             period by the book value of the net fixed assets.

  Total Debt to     See debt ratio
  Total Assets
  Ratio

p.01/14/00
G-10                                                                              GLOSSARY




  Unsystematic        The amount of total risk that can be eliminated by diversification by
  Risk                creating a portfolio. Also known as asset-specific risk or
                      company-specific risk.

  Valuation           The process of estimating the value of financial assets or
                      securities

  Variance            The weighted average of the squared deviations from the
                      expected value

  Volatility          The probability of change

  Weighted            The weighted average of the costs of the capital components
  Average Cost        (debt, preferred stock, and common stock)
  of Capital
  (WACC)

  Yield Curve         A graphical representation of the level of interest rates for

  Yield to Maturity   The measure of the average rate of return that will be earned on a
                      debt security held until it matures

  Zero-coupon         A security that makes no interest payments; it is sold at a discount
  Bond                at issue and then repaid at face value at maturity
